Common Data Environments (CDE), digital project 
management and analytical dashboards.
•	
AI and Digital Public Infrastructure: Government 
initiatives relating to artificial intelligence, geospatial 
infrastructure and digital public platforms further 
support technology adoption. India’s AI-compute 
ecosystem, geospatial-policy reforms and increasing 
adoption of digital platforms across public programmes 
are enabling data, engineering and location 
intelligence to become more closely integrated across 
infrastructure lifecycles.
The significance of public capital expenditure is 
therefore increasingly determined not only by the 
quantity of physical infrastructure being created, 
but also by the digital intensity of infrastructure 
investment. As asset owners seek greater visibility over 
cost, schedule, quality, performance and service levels, 
technology-enabled engineering and infrastructure 
intelligence are expected to become more deeply 
embedded in project delivery.
INDUSTRY STRUCTURE AND DEVELOPMENTS
A. GEOSPATIAL
Industry Overview
Geospatial technology is undergoing a fundamental 
change in its role within infrastructure and enterprise 
decision-making. Geographic Information Systems were 
historically viewed primarily as specialist mapping systems. 
Increasingly, location intelligence is becoming part of the 
underlying digital infrastructure used to plan investments, 
manage networks, monitor assets and analyse change.
The underlying geospatial data ecosystem is expanding 
rapidly. Earth Observation satellites, UAVs, aerial sensors, 
mobile mapping systems, LiDAR and terrestrial scanners are 
creating increasingly frequent and detailed representations 
of the physical world. Improved computing and cloud 
infrastructure are simultaneously enabling these datasets 
to be processed at much larger scale.
As a result, value is progressively shifting from data 
collection towards interpretation, intelligence and decision 
support.
Policy and Regulatory Environment
India’s policy environment continues to support wider 
adoption of geospatial technologies.
The National Geospatial Policy, 2022 seeks to expand 
access to geospatial information, promote innovation, 
strengthen domestic capability and increase private-
sector participation. It builds on the liberalisation measures 
introduced in 2021, which removed prior-approval 
requirements for a broad range of geospatial activities by 
Indian entities, subject to prescribed conditions.
The direction of policy is therefore towards greater 
availability and commercial use of geospatial information 
while simultaneously strengthening domestic capability.
National Geospatial Infrastructure
Policy implementation is progressing through initiatives 
such as Operation Dronagiri, launched alongside geospatial-
data-infrastructure initiatives to demonstrate geospatial 
use cases across states including Uttar Pradesh, Haryana, 
Assam, Andhra Pradesh and Maharashtra.
The Department of Science & Technology’s National 
Geospatial Programme encompasses geospatial science 
and technology, analytics and intelligence, policy, solution 
development, capacity building, entrepreneurship and 
international cooperation.
PM GatiShakti represents another important demonstration 
of mainstream geospatial adoption. The use of thousands 
of spatial data layers for integrated infrastructure planning 
indicates that geospatial information is increasingly 
becoming part of the decision architecture of major capital 
programmes rather than serving only as an output of 
individual mapping activities.
Geospatial Industry Trends for FY2025–26
•	
Expansion of Earth Observation: Earth Observation 
satellites are providing increasingly frequent imagery 
for agriculture, infrastructure, urban development, 
disaster management, environmental monitoring and 
natural-resource assessment. Improvements in spatial, 
temporal and spectral resolution are broadening the 
range of decisions that can be supported through 
satellite-derived information.
•	
Growth of Reality Capture: LiDAR, UAV photogrammetry, 
terrestrial laser scanning and mobile mapping are 
increasing the speed and level of detail with which 
physical environments can be captured. Reality-
capture datasets can support design, engineering, asset 
inventory, digital twins and change monitoring across 
infrastructure and facilities.
•	
GeoAI and Automated Spatial Intelligence: Artificial 
intelligence and machine learning are increasingly 
being applied to large geospatial datasets. Potential 
applications include automated feature extraction from 
imagery and point clouds, object classification, change 
detection, anomaly identification, infrastructure-
condition assessment, vegetation analysis and natural-
resource monitoring.
LiDAR point clouds containing millions or billions 
of observations can increasingly be segmented 
automatically to identify buildings, roads, utilities, 
poles and vegetation. Similarly, imagery from different 
dates can be processed to identify construction, 
environmental changes or encroachments.
•	
GIS and Engineering Convergence: Geospatial 
information is increasingly being integrated with 
engineering data. BIM provides detailed engineered-
asset information, while GIS supplies the surrounding 
geographic and network context. Their convergence 
enables a broader digital representation of 
infrastructure from planning through construction and 
asset management.
•	
Digital Twins: Digital twins are evolving from 3D 
visualisation towards dynamic representations 
combining 3D geometry, BIM, GIS, asset records, IoT feeds, 
operating systems and analytics. During construction, 
they can support design review and progress monitoring; 
during operations, they can support inspection, scenario 
analysis and predictive maintenance.
	
Private-sector research estimates India’s digital-twin 
market at approximately USD 762 million in 2024 
and USD 3.79 billion by 2030, implying a CAGR of 
approximately 30.6%. As definitions and methodologies 
differ among research providers, these figures should 
be viewed as directional indicators rather than precise 
measurements.
•	
AI Compute and Cloud Infrastructure: Greater 
availability of high-performance computing and cloud 
infrastructure is reducing barriers to processing large 
spatial datasets. India’s public AI-compute initiative 
is designed to broaden access to AI computing 
resources for startups, academia, MSMEs and industry, 
potentially supporting greater adoption of computer 
vision, simulation and spatial AI.
•	
Real-Time Spatial Data: Integration of IoT and 
operational data with GIS is creating increasingly 
dynamic 
location-based 
systems. 
Utilities, 
transportation networks, cities and industrial assets 
can combine static geospatial information with live 
sensor data to improve situational awareness and 
operational decision-making.
Key Growth Drivers
•	
Infrastructure Digitalisation: The growing use of 
geospatial platforms across infrastructure planning 
and asset management is expanding location 
intelligence into mainstream engineering and 
operational workflows.
•	
Policy Liberalisation: India’s geospatial policy 
framework encourages greater private-sector 
participation and innovation.
•	
Increasing Data Availability: Satellite imagery, drones, 
LiDAR, mobile mapping and IoT are generating larger 
and more frequent datasets.
•	
Artificial Intelligence: AI is reducing the effort required 
to extract information from large geospatial datasets 
and enabling more scalable analysis.
•	
Cross-sector Adoption: Water, power, transportation, 
cities, agriculture, telecom and environmental 
management increasingly depend on spatial 
information.
•	
Digital Twin Adoption: Integration of GIS, BIM, reality 
capture and operational data is creating new lifecycle 
asset-management applications.
Market estimates for the wider geospatial economy 
remain positive, although definitions vary significantly. 
One widely cited industry forecast estimates 
approximately USD 681 billion in 2025 and USD 1.44 
trillion by 2030. Rather than treating a single estimate 
as a precise measure, the more defensible conclusion is 
that major forecasts continue to anticipate substantial 
expansion of geospatial applications through the end 
of the decade.
Challenges
The increasing importance of geospatial information also 
introduces challenges.
Data Quality and Consistency: Spatial analytics and digital 
twins depend heavily on accurate and current source 
information. Incomplete asset inventories, inconsistent 
coordinate systems and outdated records can reduce the 
reliability of outputs.
Interoperability: Infrastructure organisations often operate 
multiple legacy and modern systems. Integrating GIS 
with engineering applications, ERP, SCADA, IoT and cloud 
systems can create substantial implementation complexity.
Cybersecurity and Data Governance: Geospatial datasets 
can include sensitive infrastructure information. Secure 
data architecture, access management and appropriate 
treatment of data sovereignty are therefore increasingly 
important.
AI Governance: A GeoAI system can scale errors as effectively 
as it scales insight. Model provenance, training-data quality, 
explainability, spatial bias and human verification are 
important considerations where AI-generated information 
may influence physical-world decisions.
Skills: Effective implementation increasingly requires 
multidisciplinary expertise combining geospatial 
technology, engineering, data science, cloud computing 
and sector-specific knowledge.
Outlook
Geospatial technology is increasingly moving from a 
specialist mapping function towards an enabling layer of 
digital infrastructure. The interaction of policy reform, Earth 
Observation, reality capture, AI, GIS, BIM, IoT and digital twins 
is expected to expand the role of spatial intelligence across 
public and private-sector decision-making.
The greatest value is likely to shift progressively towards 
systems that continuously interpret, update and 
operationalise geospatial information rather than merely 
collect and display it.
B. ARCHITECTURE, ENGINEERING & CONSTRUCTION, 
BIM AND TRANSPORTATION INFRASTRUCTURE
Infrastructure Investment and Digital Delivery
India’s Architecture, Engineering and Construction 
ecosystem continues to benefit from sustained public 
infrastructure expenditure, urbanisation, industrial 
development and technology adoption.

Annual Report 2025-26
Large infrastructure programmes increasingly involve 
multiple disciplines, consultants, contractors and 
authorities. Traditional dependence on disconnected 
drawings, spreadsheets, documents and project systems 
makes coordination more difficult as programme complexity 
increases.
Digital delivery is therefore becoming progressively 
important across design, construction and lifecycle 
management.
BIM and Common Data Environments
Building Information Modelling is evolving from a design 
technology into a broader information-management 
environment. BIM provides a structured digital 
representation of an engineered asset, while a Common 
Data Environment provides controlled access to project 
documents, models and related information.
This supports improved coordination across project teams, 
reduces duplication and provides greater control over 
versions, approvals and information exchange.
4D and 5D Project Management
Integration of BIM with programme schedules enables 4D 
construction planning, allowing teams to visualise project 
sequencing and understand how construction should 
progress over time.
Adding quantity and cost information creates 5D workflows, 
enabling project teams to assess how design and schedule 
changes may influence quantities and financial outcomes.
Digital project-management platforms can further integrate 
dashboards, progress information, issues and approvals to 
improve management visibility.
GIS–BIM Integration
BIM describes the engineered asset, while GIS places that 
asset within its geographic and network context.
Their integration is particularly important for large 
infrastructure systems such as highways, metros, railways, 
utilities and urban developments, where land, existing 
infrastructure, environmental conditions and neighbouring 
assets materially affect project decisions.
The combination enables information to move more 
effectively from survey and planning through design, 
construction and operations.
Reality Capture and Construction Monitoring
UAVs, LiDAR, terrestrial scanning and photogrammetry are 
increasingly being used to capture existing conditions and 
monitor construction.
Repeated reality capture can provide objective evidence 
of site progress and enable comparison between planned 
and actual conditions. At completion, the resulting data can 
contribute to digital asset records and future operations and 
maintenance.
Transportation Infrastructure
Transportation is particularly suited to integrated geospatial 
and digital-engineering approaches because roads, railways, 
metros, airports and ports are spatially extensive assets with 
long lifecycles.
GIS supports route planning, utility and land analysis, 
environmental assessment and network-level management. 
BIM supports multidisciplinary engineering, while CDE and 
project-control systems provide structured environments 
for implementation.
Intelligent Traffic Management Systems (IMTS)
India’s transportation infrastructure is increasingly 
incorporating Intelligent Traffic Management Systems 
(ITMS) to improve traffic flow, road safety, enforcement and 
real-time network management. ITMS deployments typically 
integrate adaptive traffic-control systems, CCTV and video 
analytics, Automatic Number Plate Recognition, red-light 
and speed-violation detection, electronic enforcement and 
centralised command-and-control platforms.
Under the Smart Cities Mission, ITMS has been integrated 
with Integrated Command and Control Centres across 
cities, enabling real-time traffic monitoring and data-
driven management. Government assessments indicate 
that these systems are supporting improved traffic 
operations, more effective enforcement of violations 
and reduction in journey times. Similar technology-led 
approaches are also being adopted on highways and 
expressways through Advanced Traffic Management 
Systems incorporating incident detection, surveillance and 
electronic enforcement.
The increasing adoption of such systems reflects a broader 
shift from conventional traffic-control infrastructure towards 
connected, data-driven transportation management, 
combining sensors, communications, GIS, video analytics, 
artificial intelligence and centralised operating platforms to 
improve mobility and road-network efficiency.
Road and Transport Asset Management
As transport networks expand, asset owners increasingly 
require structured information on pavements, structures, 
roadside assets, safety systems and maintenance history.
Road Asset Management Systems can integrate condition 
information with GIS and engineering analysis, enabling 
authorities to prioritise interventions and allocate 
maintenance budgets based on asset condition and 
lifecycle requirements.
Key Growth Drivers
•	
Sustained Infrastructure Expenditure: Continued 
investment across highways, railways, metros, ports 
and urban infrastructure supports engineering and 
project-management requirements.
•	
Growing Programme Complexity: Multi-stakeholder 
projects require better information governance, 
coordination and progress visibility.
•	
Shift towards Digital Delivery: BIM, CDE, 4D/5D planning 
and digital project management are increasingly 
relevant to major infrastructure programmes.
•	
Lifecycle Asset Management: Infrastructure owners 
are increasingly focused not only on construction but 
also on the long-term condition, performance and 
maintenance of assets.
Challenges
Key challenges include fragmented legacy systems, limited 
interoperability across technology platforms, inconsistent 
data standards, differing levels of digital maturity across 
project participants, project approval cycles and the 
requirement to develop multidisciplinary skills capable of 
working across engineering and digital environments.
C. WATER AND WASTEWATER
Sector Overview
India’s water sector is entering a new phase of development. 
While significant investment in physical infrastructure 
remains necessary, the focus is progressively broadening 
from asset creation towards reliable service delivery, network 
efficiency, source sustainability, wastewater management, 
monitoring and long-term operations.
Expanding Water Supply Coverage
As of end of FY 2025-26, approximately 15.69 crore of 
India’s 19.36 crore rural households, or 81.02%, had tap-
water connections under the Jal Jeevan Mission. This 
represented approximately 12.56 crore additional household 
connections from the programme’s 2019 baseline.
As coverage increases, the sector’s priorities are evolving. 
Greater emphasis is required on whether water reaches 
consumers reliably, at appropriate pressure and quality, 
and whether the infrastructure created can be operated 
and maintained sustainably.
Jal Jeevan Mission itself extends beyond pipeline 
construction. Its framework includes source sustainability, 
recharge, reuse, greywater management, conservation 
and rainwater harvesting. The next phase of infrastructure 
development therefore increasingly requires measurement, 
data management and lifecycle operation alongside civil 
infrastructure.
Water Distribution and Network Modernisation
Urbanisation and expansion of service areas are creating 
continuing requirements for pipeline rehabilitation, pressure 
management, asset mapping and network optimisation. Many 
utilities operate infrastructure developed over several decades, 
often with incomplete or inconsistent digital asset records.
GIS-linked network information can provide structured 
records of pipelines, valves, pumping stations, reservoirs, 
treatment plants and service connections together with 
information on capacity, condition and maintenance history. 
Integration of these records with operational systems can 
improve understanding of how networks perform in real 
operating conditions.
Non-Revenue Water and Efficiency
Reduction of Non-Revenue Water (NRW) remains an 
important priority. Water losses can arise from physical 
leakage, inaccurate metering, illegal connections, incomplete 
consumer indexing and deficiencies in network information.
Modern NRW programmes increasingly combine network 
surveys, consumer indexing, GIS, hydraulic modelling, 
flow and pressure measurement, District Metered Areas, 
leak detection and operational analytics. The objective is 
progressively moving from identifying losses at an aggregate 
level to locating and addressing them systematically.
Digital Water Management
Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition (SCADA), IoT and 
telemetry are becoming increasingly relevant to water 
operations. Real-time monitoring of flow, pressure, reservoir 
levels, pumping operations and water quality can enable 
utilities to detect abnormal conditions more rapidly and 
make more informed operating decisions.
Integrated dashboards and centralised command 
environments can combine information from multiple 
assets and systems, providing operators with improved 
visibility across large networks.
Wastewater Treatment and Reuse
Urban water programmes demonstrate increasing 
emphasis on sewerage, treatment and reuse alongside 
freshwater supply. AMRUT 2.0 includes conventional 
network investments as well as initiatives involving 24x7 
water supply, SCADA and treated-water reuse.
Public project records include 24x7 water-supply and 
SCADA augmentation in Navi Mumbai and a 42 MLD 
treated-wastewater-reuse project in Vadodara, illustrating 
the growing importance of resource efficiency and reuse 
within urban water planning.
AI, Analytics and Digital Twins
Once reliable network and operating data are available, 
artificial intelligence and analytics can support anomaly 
detection, demand forecasting, leakage identification, 
pressure optimisation, predictive maintenance and 
prioritisation of capital expenditure.
Digital twins can extend these capabilities by integrating 
hydraulic models, spatial information and operational 
data into dynamic representations of water networks. 
This enables utilities to simulate scenarios, understand 
the effects of operational changes and potentially move 
towards more predictive forms of asset management.
Sector Outlook
The longer-term water opportunity extends across 
the complete asset lifecycle: water-resource planning, 
engineering surveys, Detailed Project Reports, hydraulic 
modelling, network digitisation, NRW reduction, project 
monitoring, instrumentation, SCADA, IoT, water-quality 
management, wastewater treatment, reuse and operations 
and maintenance.

Climate variability, population growth, urbanisation and 
competition for water resources reinforce the need for more 
efficient management. However, implementation remains 
dependent on public funding, project approvals, local utility 
capability and the ability to maintain digital infrastructure 
after commissioning.
D. ENERGY AND UTILITIES
India’s power sector is expanding while simultaneously 
becoming more decentralised, data-intensive and 
operationally complex.
Renewable Energy Expansion
As of July 31, 2026, Ministry of New and Renewable Energy 
data indicated approximately 164.6 GW of solar capacity 
and 58.1 GW of wind capacity. Renewable capacity 
including large hydro stood at approximately 291.7 GW, 
while total non-fossil capacity including nuclear power was 
approximately 300.5 GW.
Continued renewable-capacity additions are changing 
the requirements of the broader electricity system. Solar 
and wind resources are geographically distributed and 
variable, increasing the need for transmission infrastructure, 
forecasting, balancing and flexible operation.
Transmission and Grid Modernisation
The Central Electricity Authority is planning transmission 
infrastructure to integrate more than 500 GW of non-fossil 
capacity by 2030, together with a longer-term transmission 
framework associated with more than 900 GW by 2035–36.
Expansion at this scale requires new transmission 
corridors, substations, network upgrades and increasingly 
sophisticated planning and monitoring systems.
Energy Storage
Energy storage is emerging as a critical component of 
renewable integration. Based on the National Electricity 
Plan, estimated storage requirements are approximately 
82.37 GWh in FY2026–27, comprising pumped-storage 
and battery-energy-storage systems, rising substantially 
to approximately 411.4 GWh by FY2031–32.
The addition of storage introduces new assets and 
operational variables, further increasing the need for 
integrated data and network management.
Utility Digitalisation
Grid modernisation increasingly requires integration among 
GIS, SCADA, Distribution Management Systems, Outage 
Management Systems, Advanced Metering Infrastructure 
and other operational data platforms.
An integrated digital environment can improve network 
visibility, support outage response, strengthen maintenance 
planning and provide operators with a more complete 
representation of the electricity network.
Geospatial Intelligence and Asset Management
Geospatial technology has important applications in 
transmission-corridor planning, right-of-way analysis, 
renewable siting, land assessment, network mapping and 
asset inventory.
LiDAR, aerial imagery and remote sensing can support 
powerline modelling, clearance analysis and infrastructure 
inspection. Enterprise GIS can enable utilities to maintain 
structured and spatially accurate records across extensive 
transmission and distribution networks.
Vegetation and Corridor Management
Vegetation management is becoming increasingly 
technology-enabled, particularly across long transmission 
and distribution corridors. Remote sensing, LiDAR and 
imagery can assist in identifying vegetation encroachments, 
analysing clearances and prioritising field inspection or 
trimming activity based on risk.
Machine-learning techniques can further assist in 
processing large datasets and identifying areas requiring 
attention.
Digital Twins and Predictive Operations
Digital twins provide an additional layer by integrating 
network geometry, engineering information, operating 
data and analytics. Potential applications include asset-
condition assessment, simulation, maintenance planning 
and predictive operations.
Their effectiveness, however, depends on the completeness, 
consistency and interoperability of underlying asset and 
operational data.
Sector Outlook
The Energy opportunity is therefore broader than renewable 
generation alone. Renewable additions, transmission 
expansion, storage, smart metering and increasing 
network complexity are creating requirements for 
energy-infrastructure digitalisation across planning, asset 
management, monitoring and operations.
E. MOBILITY ENGINEERING
Automotive Industry Performance
Global Automotive and Mobility Trends
The global automotive industry is undergoing a significant 
engineering transformation driven by electrification, 
software-defined vehicles, connectivity, automation and 
increasingly digital manufacturing environments. These 
changes are increasing the engineering content of vehicles 
and reshaping the way automotive products are designed, 
validated and manufactured.
Electrification remains one of the most significant global 
trends. According to the International Energy Agency, 
global electric-car sales exceeded 20 million units in 
2025, representing growth of around 20% year on year and 
approximately one-quarter of global car sales. The transition 
is creating new requirements across battery systems, 
thermal management, lightweight structures, power 
electronics, vehicle packaging and charging infrastructure.
At the same time, the increasing software and electronics 
content of vehicles is accelerating the development of 
software-defined and connected vehicle architectures. 
Embedded systems, electronic control systems, digital 
interfaces, connectivity and advanced validation are 
becoming increasingly important components of 
automotive engineering.
Automotive manufacturers globally are also increasing 
the use of simulation, virtual validation, Product Lifecycle 
Management and digital manufacturing to shorten 
development cycles and reduce reliance on physical 
prototypes. Robotics, manufacturing-process simulation, 
virtual commissioning and factory automation are becoming 
increasingly integrated into production engineering.
These trends are expanding the scope of mobility 
engineering beyond conventional mechanical design 
towards a multidisciplinary environment combining 
mechanical engineering, electronics, software, simulation 
and advanced manufacturing.
Indian Automotive Industry Performance
India’s automotive industry remained robust during 
FY2025–26. According to the Society of Indian Automobile 
Manufacturers, domestic vehicle sales were approximately 
28.27 million units, compared with approximately 25.61 
million units in FY2024–25, representing growth of roughly 
10.4%. Total vehicle production was approximately 34.71 
million units, while exports were about 6.65 million units.
Initial FY2026–27 industry data also remained positive, 
with growth across passenger vehicles, two-wheelers, 
commercial vehicles and three-wheelers, although 
commodity costs and geopolitical developments remain 
areas of concern.
Electrification in India
In India, electric-car sales increased by approximately 
75% to around 165,000 units in 2025, approaching 4% of 
domestic car sales.
Electrification is significantly further advanced in smaller 
vehicle categories. India sold just under 1.3 million electric 
two-wheelers in 2025, representing around 6% of two-
wheeler sales, while electric three-wheelers approached 
800,000 units and accounted for more than two-thirds of 
three-wheeler sales. The pace of transition therefore differs 
materially across vehicle categories.
Policy Support – PM E-DRIVE
The PM E-DRIVE scheme, with an overall outlay of 
approximately `11,900 crore, supports electric two- and three-
wheelers, buses, trucks, ambulances, testing infrastructure 
and charging. Approximately `2,000 crore is allocated to 
public charging infrastructure, with the tenure for specified 
components and categories extended to March 31, 2028.
Product Engineering Transformation
Electrification and new vehicle architectures are creating 
engineering requirements around battery integration, 
thermal management, lightweight structures, power 
electronics, vehicle packaging and validation.
The interaction between mechanical, electrical, 
electronic and software systems is increasing the need 
for multidisciplinary engineering across the product-
development lifecycle.
Software-defined and Connected Vehicles
Globally, automotive development is progressively 
moving towards greater software and electronics content. 
Embedded systems, electronic architectures, connectivity, 
digital interfaces and system-level validation are becoming 
increasingly important elements of vehicle development.
This shift is expanding the engineering effort required 
across integration, testing, validation and lifecycle software 
management.
Simulation and Virtual Validation
Simulation and virtual validation are increasingly being used 
to shorten product-development cycles, improve design 
maturity and reduce dependence on physical prototypes.
Relevant engineering disciplines include crash simulation, 
Noise, Vibration and Harshness, durability, multi-body 
dynamics, Computational Fluid Dynamics, thermal analysis 
and manufacturing simulation.
Digital Manufacturing and Factory Automation
Manufacturing engineering is also becoming increasingly 
digital. Automotive and industrial manufacturers are 
investing in process simulation, line balancing, robotic 
simulation, virtual commissioning, automation and digital 
manufacturing.
Product Lifecycle Management systems provide a digital 
thread across product definition, configuration and 
manufacturing information, helping improve continuity 
from design to production.
Key Growth Drivers
•	
Global Electrification: EV adoption continues to reshape 
vehicle architecture and engineering requirements.
•	
Software and Electronics: Increasing software content 
is expanding embedded, connected and validation 
requirements.
•	
Virtual Engineering: Simulation and digital validation 
are becoming more important as manufacturers seek 
shorter development cycles.
•	
Digital Manufacturing: Robotics, automation, 
virtual commissioning and process simulation are 
transforming manufacturing engineering.
•	
Indian Automotive Growth: India’s large domestic 
automotive market and growing production base 
provide an additional demand driver.

•	
Engineering Complexity: Increasing interaction 
between mechanical, electrical, electronics and 
software systems is raising engineering intensity 
across vehicle programmes.
Challenges
The sector remains exposed to cyclical automotive demand, 
commodity-price movements, changing consumer 
preferences and differences in EV-adoption rates across 
regions and vehicle segments.
Automotive manufacturers also face continuing pressure 
to manage product-development costs while investing 
simultaneously in electrification, software, electronics 
and manufacturing transformation. Rapid technological 
evolution can require frequent investment in new tools, 
platforms and specialist engineering skills.
Outlook
The global automotive industry is expected to continue 
transitioning towards electrified, connected, software-
intensive and digitally engineered products. At the same 
time, India remains an important automotive production 
and consumption market.
The Mobility Engineering opportunity is therefore 
increasingly driven not only by growth in vehicle volumes 
but by the rising engineering complexity of vehicles 
and manufacturing systems globally. Electrification, 
embedded systems, simulation, virtual validation, PLM 
and manufacturing automation are expected to remain 
important areas of engineering transformation.
F. DATA CENTRES, AI COMPUTE AND DIGITAL 
INFRASTRUCTURE
Market Expansion
India’s data-centre industry continues to expand as cloud 
adoption, enterprise digitalisation and artificial-intelligence 
workloads increase demand for computing capacity.
Industry estimates vary according to methodology. CBRE 
estimated operational capacity at approximately 1,530 
MW by September 2025, while Cushman & Wakefield 
subsequently estimated approximately 1.6 GW of operational 
capacity in 2026, with substantial additional capacity under 
construction or planned.
Given differences in definitions, the broad conclusion is 
that India has moved into approximately the 1.5–1.6 GW 
operational-capacity range, with a significant development 
pipeline.
AI-led Compute Demand
Artificial intelligence is an important incremental demand 
driver. Training and inference workloads require high-
density computing, increasing requirements for reliable 
power, cooling, network connectivity and energy efficiency.
The AI investment cycle therefore has important 
implications for physical infrastructure as well as computing 
infrastructure.
Infrastructure Requirements
Large data-centre facilities require reliable grid connections, 
cooling and water systems, land, fibre connectivity, 
engineering infrastructure and operational monitoring.
Site-selection decisions increasingly involve assessment of 
power availability, environmental constraints, connectivity, 
resilience and other geographic factors.
Digital Engineering and Asset Management
As data-centre campuses increase in scale and complexity, 
BIM, digital twins, CDEs, reality capture and asset-
information systems can support design, construction, 
commissioning and subsequent operations.
The sector therefore creates adjacent requirements 
across engineering, geospatial analysis and lifecycle asset 
management.
Sustainability and Resource Constraints
Power availability, grid capacity, water consumption, 
land availability and sustainability requirements remain 
important considerations. As AI-led compute requirements 
increase, operators will face continued pressure to improve 
energy efficiency and integrate renewable power.
Outlook
India’s continued digitalisation and the increasing adoption 
of cloud and AI technologies are expected to support 
additional data-centre investment. However, resource 
availability, infrastructure capacity and sustainability 
considerations will remain critical to project development.
OPPORTUNITIES
The developments across infrastructure, utilities, geospatial 
technologies, mobility and digital infrastructure point to a 
number of interconnected opportunities.
•	
Digitalisation of Physical Infrastructure: Roads, 
railways, metros, water systems, power networks and 
urban infrastructure increasingly require a digital 
information layer alongside physical construction. GIS, 
BIM, CDEs, digital project management, reality capture 
and analytical dashboards can create a digital thread 
from planning through execution and operations.
•	
Digitally Managed Water Systems: As water-supply 
coverage expands, the focus is increasingly shifting 
towards service reliability, NRW reduction, pressure and 
flow monitoring, water-quality assurance, wastewater 
reuse and long-term operations. Integration of 
engineering with GIS, hydraulic modelling, SCADA, IoT 
and analytics can support this transition.
•	
Grid Modernisation and Energy Intelligence: 
Renewable capacity additions, transmission expansion, 
distributed assets and storage are increasing the 
complexity of electricity networks. Network GIS, 
remote inspection, geospatial siting, asset intelligence 
and operational analytics can support planning and 
management of these systems.
•	
GeoAI and Spatial Automation: Increasing availability 
of Earth Observation, LiDAR, drone and reality-capture 
data creates substantial opportunities for automated 
feature extraction, change detection, classification 
and condition assessment. Artificial intelligence can 
potentially reduce the time required to convert large 
spatial datasets into usable infrastructure intelligence.
•	
Digital Twins and Lifecycle Asset Management: 
Integration of GIS, BIM, IoT and operational data 
creates opportunities to maintain dynamic digital 
representations of infrastructure and assets. These 
systems can support progress monitoring, scenario 
analysis, maintenance planning and operational 
decision-making across multiple industries.
•	
Mobility Engineering Transformation: Electrification, 
embedded systems, software, simulation and 
manufacturing automation continue to increase the 
engineering intensity of vehicle programmes. These 
changes create requirements for multidisciplinary 
product-development, validation and manufacturing 
capabilities.
•	
AI-led Digital Infrastructure: Growth in AI computing 
is increasing demand for data centres and associated 
power, cooling, connectivity and engineering 
infrastructure. This also supports adoption of cloud-
based analytics, computer vision and engineering 
automation in other sectors.
•	
Cross-sector Technology Convergence: Increasingly, 
infrastructure problems cannot be addressed through 
individual technologies in isolation. Geospatial 
information, engineering, sensors, cloud systems, 
AI and analytics are converging into integrated 
operational environments, creating opportunities for 
multidisciplinary solutions.
THREATS AND INDUSTRY CHALLENGES
The positive structural environment is accompanied by 
several challenges that could affect the pace of industry 
growth and project implementation.
•	
Extended Procurement Cycles: Large public-
infrastructure projects can involve extended periods of 
tendering, technical evaluation, commercial evaluation, 
administrative approvals and contractual finalisation. 
Consequently, the timing of industry opportunities can 
remain difficult to predict.
•	
Increasing Competitive Intensity: Engineering 
companies, IT service providers, specialist GIS firms, 
software OEMs, consulting organisations and new AI-led 
companies are increasingly competing across overlapping 
areas of the infrastructure-technology value chain.
•	
Rapid Technology Obsolescence: Artificial intelligence, 
cloud architectures, sensors, engineering platforms 
and software environments are evolving quickly. 
Organisations must continuously evaluate emerging 
technologies and maintain relevant technical skills.
•	
Data Quality and Legacy Systems: Digital twins, 
analytics and GeoAI rely on reliable source information. 
Incomplete asset records, inconsistent data standards 
and fragmented legacy systems can increase 
implementation effort and reduce the quality of digital 
outputs.
•	
Cybersecurity and Data Sovereignty: Infrastructure 
systems increasingly contain sensitive operational 
and spatial data. Greater connectivity creates 
additional requirements around cybersecurity, access 
management, traceability and data residency.
•	
AI Governance: Automated analysis can propagate 
incorrect or biased outputs at scale. Model quality, 
provenance, explainability and human validation will 
become increasingly important where AI supports 
engineering or infrastructure decisions.
•	
Specialised Talent Availability: The convergence of 
engineering, GIS, cloud, AI, automation and domain 
knowledge increases demand for multidisciplinary 
professionals whose skills may take considerable time 
to develop.
•	
Macroeconomic and Geopolitical Volatility: Energy 
prices, trade restrictions, exchange-rate movements 
and geopolitical conflicts can affect investment 
decisions, input costs and international project 
activity.
•	
Automotive Market Cyclicality: Automotive engineering 
remains influenced by underlying vehicle demand and 
customer product-development cycles. Changes in EV 
adoption, technology investment or product strategies 
may influence engineering expenditure.
These factors imply that while the structural opportunity 
across infrastructure and digital engineering remains 
substantial, the pace of growth will continue to depend 
on investment decisions, execution capability, technology 
development and customer procurement cycles.
OUTLOOK
Infrastructure and Public Investment Outlook
India enters FY2026–27 following strong economic growth 
and with continued public support for infrastructure 
investment. Effective capital expenditure of approximately 
`17.15 lakh crore, continuing investment in transportation 
and wider use of PM GatiShakti provide a supportive 
environment for infrastructure development.
More significantly, infrastructure programmes are 
increasingly expected to incorporate digital planning, 
monitoring and asset information alongside conventional 
engineering.
Geospatial and Digital Engineering Outlook
Geospatial technology is expected to become more 
deeply embedded in infrastructure, utilities and enterprise 
operations. The combination of Earth Observation, reality 
capture, GIS, BIM, AI and digital twins is expanding

spatial intelligence from mapping towards automated 
interpretation and lifecycle decision-making.
GeoAI is expected to become particularly important as the 
volume and frequency of spatial data continue to increase.
Utilities and Sustainability Outlook
Water and power infrastructure are becoming increasingly 
data-intensive. In Water, expanding network coverage 
is increasing the importance of operations, monitoring, 
efficiency and reuse. In Energy, renewable integration, 
transmission expansion and storage are increasing network 
complexity.
These trends are expected to support continued adoption of 
digital asset management, SCADA, IoT, geospatial analysis 
and predictive analytics.
Mobility and Digital Infrastructure Outlook
India’s automotive sector continues to benefit from a 
substantial domestic market, while electrification, software-
defined architectures and manufacturing automation are 
reshaping engineering requirements.
Simultaneously, data-centre and AI infrastructure 
investment is creating additional demand for power, cooling, 
engineering and digital asset-management systems.
Overall, the FY2026–27 industry outlook remains 
constructive. However, the environment is not without 
uncertainty. Geopolitical conditions, procurement 
timelines, technology cycles, financing costs and customer 
investment decisions will continue to influence the timing 
of opportunities.
RISKS & CONCERNS
Effective risk management is critical to sustaining 
business performance and protecting shareholder value. 
The Company has established a robust Risk Management 
framework that ensures adequate controls, continuous 
monitoring, and proactive mitigation of potential threats. 
This framework helps identify, assess, and prioritize risks 
based on their likelihood and potential impact, enabling the 
Company to respond strategically.
Beyond the risk factors mentioned elsewhere in this report, 
the following key risks could materially impact on actual 
results versus expectations:
•	
Regulatory Changes: Alterations in domestic and 
foreign policies, including laws, regulations, and tax 
regimes.
•	
Foreign Exchange Volatility: Fluctuations in currency 
exchange rates affecting costs and revenues.
•	
Licensing & Approvals: Failure to secure or renew 
essential approvals and licenses.
•	
Contingent Liabilities: Realization of unanticipated 
legal or contractual obligations.
•	
Macroeconomic & Political Instability: Shifts in 
economic, political, or social conditions at local, 
regional, or national levels.
•	
Client Contract Risks: Termination of contracts without 
cause, with little or no notice, or with heavy penalties.
•	
Interest Rate Movements: Variations in interest, 
inflation, or deflation rates impacting financing and 
operating costs.
•	
Natural Disasters & Calamities: Events disrupting 
operations in affected geographies.
•	
Market Dynamics: Unpredictable fluctuations in 
industry demand, competition, and technology trends 
beyond the Company’s control.
The Company remains committed to continuously 
enhancing its risk management capabilities to anticipate 
emerging threats and adapt to evolving market conditions
INTERNAL CONTROL AND SYSTEMS AND THEIR 
ADEQUACY
As defined under Section 134(5) of the Companies Act, 2013, 
the Internal Financial Control (IFC) framework comprises 
the policies and procedures adopted by the Company to 
ensure orderly and efficient business conduct. This includes 
adherence to the Company’s policies, safeguarding of assets, 
prevention and detection of frauds and errors, accuracy and 
completeness of accounting records, and timely preparation 
of reliable financial information.
Ceinsys has fully aligned its internal control framework 
with the requirements of the Companies Act, 2013. 
The system is designed to enhance transparency and 
accountability in operations, with robust mechanisms to 
identify, assess, and mitigate risks. The framework lays 
down detailed processes to ensure its effectiveness. The 
controls are commensurate with the size and nature of the 
Company’s operations and provide reasonable assurance 
regarding the accuracy and reliability of financial and 
operational information, compliance with applicable laws, 
and safeguarding of assets from unauthorized use. A clearly 
defined delegation of authority framework governs revenue 
and expenditure approvals, while processes for annual and 
long-term business planning are in place. The Company 
utilizes advanced ERP systems to consolidate and manage 
data across locations, ensuring efficient information 
exchange and alignment with global best practices. An 
Internal Auditor, reporting to the Audit Committee, oversees 
the review of internal controls and operational risks in 
areas such as software delivery, accounting and finance, 
procurement, employee engagement, travel, insurance, and 
IT processes. The Audit Committee evaluates internal audit 
reports, discusses observations with statutory auditors, 
and follows up on corrective actions. Regular updates are 
provided to the Board on the adequacy and effectiveness 
of the Company’s internal control systems.
FINANCIAL PERFORMANCE WITH RESPECT TO OPERATIONAL PERFORMANCE

in all material respects with the Accounting Standards specified under Section 133 of the Companies Act, 2013 read with 
Rule 7 of the Companies (Accounts) Rules, 2014 and the relevant provisions of the Companies Act, 2013.

Other Current assets
On Standalone basis, total other current assets were of 
` 792 Lakhs as at March 31, 2026 as compared to total other 
current assets of ` 1,566 Lakhs as at March 31, 2025.
On Consolidated basis, total other current assets as at March 
31, 2026 stood at `860 Lakhs as against ` 1,644 Lakhs as 
at March 31, 2025.
This decrease are mainly on account of decrease in advance 
to suppliers and decrease in balances with government 
authorities.
Total Revenue- Standalone
Total Revenue from operations on Standalone basis in 
financial year 2025-26 increased to ` 63,517 Lakhs from 
` 39,973 Lakhs in the last financial year 2024-25 registering 
an increase of 59%.
The revenue has gone up due to receipt of new contract 
and execution of existing and new contracts in FY 25-26.
Total Revenue – Consolidated
Total Revenue from operations on consolidated basis in 
financial year 2025-26 increased to ` 66,070 Lakhs from 
` 41,806 Lakhs in the last financial year 2024-25 an increase 
rate of 58%.
The revenue has gone up due to receipt of new contract 
and execution of existing and new contracts in FY 25-26.
Earnings before interest, tax and depreciation (EBITD) – 
Standalone
Earnings before interest, tax and depreciation (EBITD) for the 
financial year 2025-26 has been `19,583 Lakhs registering 
an increase of 65% over (EBITD) of ` 11,873 Lakhs in 
financial year 2024-25.
Increase is on account of receipt of new contract and 
execution of existing and new contracts in FY 25-26.
Earnings before interest, tax and depreciation (EBITD) – 
Consolidated
Earnings before interest, tax and depreciation (EBITD) for the 
financial year 2025-26 has been ` 17,375 Lakhs registering 
an increase of 73% over (EBITD) of ` 10,028 Lakhs in financial 
year 2024-25.
Profit Before tax (PBT) – Standalone
Profit before tax for the financial year 2025-26 has been 
` 18,260 Lakhs recording an increase of 65% over the PBT 
of ` 11,081 Lakhs in financial year 2024-25.
Increase is on account of increase in revenue.
Profit Before Tax (PBT) – Consolidated
Profit before tax for the financial year 2025-26 has been 
` 15,606 Lakhs recording an increase of 74% over the PBT 
of ` 8,954 Lakhs in financial year 2024-25.
The variance is on account of increase in Revenue from 
Operations during the year.
Profit After Tax (PAT) – Standalone
Profit after tax (PAT) for the financial year 2025-26 has been 
`15,421 Lakhs recording an increase of 89% over the PAT of 
` 8,138 Lakhs in financial year 2024-25.
The variance is on account of increase in Revenue from 
Operations during the year.
Profit After Tax (PAT) – Consolidated
Profit after tax (PAT) for the financial year 2025-26 was 
` 13,342 Lakhs recording an increase of 111% over the Profit 
after tax (PAT) of ` 6,324 Lakhs in financial year 2024-25.
The variance is on account of increase in Revenue from 
Operations during the year.
Net worth
On Standalone basis, Net worth for financial year 2025-26 
was recorded at ` 68,568 Lakhs as compared to ` 40,425 
Lakhs in previous financial year 2024-25. The net increase 
of ` 28,143 Lakhs is mainly due to increase in profitability 
of FY 2025-26 and due to capital Infusion during the year.
On Consolidated basis, Net worth for financial year 2025-26 
was recorded at ` 67,306 Lakhs as compared to ` 41,188 
Lakhs in previous FY 2024-25. The net increase of ` 26,118 
Lakhs is mainly due to increase in profitability of FY 2025-26 
and due to capital Infusion during the year.
Earnings per Share (EPS)
On Standalone basis, Basic earnings per share went up to 
` 86.41 per share in current financial year from ` 48.09 per 
share in the previous year. For diluted earning per share 
went up to ` 78.44 per share in current financial year from 
` 45.39 per share in the previous year.
On Consolidated basis, Basic earnings per share went up 
to ` 74.76 per share in current financial year from ` 37.37 
per share in the previous year. For diluted earning per share 
went up to ` 67.87 per share in current financial year from 
` 35.27 per share in the previous year.
On Standalone Level there is an increase in EPS on account 
of increase in profit after tax due to the reasons mentioned 
above.
On Consolidated level, there is increase in EPS on account 
of increase in profit after tax due to the reasons mentioned 
above.
Moreover, in year 2025-26 basic and diluted EPS are not 
the same as there were outstanding potential share during 
the year.
Segment Wise performance
The Company has organised its business into three 
segments:
1.	
Geospatial & Engineering Services–This segment 
includes Geospatial solution for Cadastral mapping, 
Resource survey, mapping & analysis, Mining 
applications & Heritage Mapping. Engineering 
consultancy services includes hydraulic modelling, 
water metering solutions, municipal solutions, 
Consultancy services for the DPR preparations for 
water supply scheme, Road and Highways using LiDAR 
Technology.
2.	
Technology Solutions – This Segment includes IoT that 
merges live sensor feeds with project data for a 360° 
view of operations, risk, and progress. Tech solutions 
on the Tech platforms of Autodesk, Bentley, Esri, Aveva 
and othe`
3.	
Others–This segment aiming to create renewable 
energy that is revolutionising and redefining the way 
sustainable energy sources are harnessed across the 
world. At present Ceinsys has invested in Wind Mills to 
generate renewable clean energy.
4.	
Unallocable consists of other income, expenses, assets 
and liabilities which cannot be directly identified to any 
of the above segments.
As per Ind AS 108 ‘Operating Segments’, the Company 
has disclosed the segment information only as part of the 
consolidated financial results.

MATERIAL DEVELOPMENTS IN HUMAN RESOURCES
HUMAN RESOURCES
At CS TECH Ai, our employees are our greatest asset and the 
cornerstone of our sustainable growth. We remain steadfast 
in fostering their professional growth, overall well-being, 
and workplace satisfaction. Guided by our core values, our 
culture champions collaboration, integrity, inclusivity, and 
innovation across all operations.
During FY 2025–26, CS TECH Ai strengthened its employee 
engagement framework through structured learning 
pathways, recognition forums, comprehensive wellness 
initiatives, and pulse surveys that validated our employee-
centric policies, transparent leadership communication, 
equitable workplace opportunities, and strong culture of 
pride and camaraderie.
Our people-first initiatives reached a defining milestone 
when CS TECH Ai was recognized as a Great Place To 
Work® Certified™ organization. This global recognition is 
a testament to our unwavering commitment to building a 
high-trust, high-performance culture where diverse talent 
can thrive, achieve excellence, and feel valued.
Core Values
•	
Collaboration: We believe in the power of working 
together, knowing that it yields inspiring results.
•	
Respect for Diverse Perspectives:  We recognise that 
varied viewpoints foster innovation and growth.
•	
Unconventional Thinking:  We encourage creativity 
and ingenuity to help us translate ambitions into reality. 
•	
Integrity: We stand on our integrity, acknowledging that 
trust and respect enable us to exceed expectations. 
•	
Responsibility:  We embrace our responsibility with 
a  profound sense of commitment to our clients, 
colleagues, and the community.
•	
Client Partnership:  We are passionate about building 
strong connections with our clients.
•	
Pride in Our People: We take pride in our employees, 
who are the core of our success.
POSH ACT & ETHICS AND SECURITY ORIENTATION
The Company maintains a strict zero-tolerance policy and 
full compliance under the Prevention of Sexual Harassment 
(POSH) Act, 2013, alongside rigorous cybersecurity and 
corporate ethics guidelines across all office locations 
and remote operations. Following our national-level 
honors in previous years, we have continued to advance 
comprehensive sensitization programs and robust internal 
redressal mechanisms to guarantee a safe, secure, and 
respectful work environment for all.
New Joiners
Demonstrating sustained operational scaling and talent 
attraction, the Company welcomed 375 new team 
members during FY 2025–26 across technical, managerial, 
and operational functions.
Key Differentiators for Women Empowerment:
•	
Inclusive Recruitment & Referral Incentive: Tailored 
referral bonuses for onboarding female talent, paired 
with focused career advancement pathways that 
have positioned women leaders across key verticals 
including PMO, Quality, HR, L&D, RMG, Technical 
Delivery, and Corporate Governance.
•	
Holistic Health & Mental Well-being: Annual health 
screenings, periodic specialized masterclasses, and 
targeted mental wellness workshops prioritized 
throughout the year.
•	
Targeted L&D Investments: Dedicated skill 
enhancement and leadership development tracks 
curated specifically to build mid- and senior-level 
pipelines for women.
•	
Comprehensive Maternity & Family Benefits: 6 
months of fully paid maternity leave, complemented 
by institutional support for IVF treatments, adoption, 
and surrogacy.
•	
‘Maher’ Welcome Back & Relocation Program: 
Seamless post-marriage location transfer policies and 
structured re-entry frameworks for women transitioning 
back into the workforce after career breaks.
•	
Equal Opportunity & Pay Parity: Strict adherence to 
equal compensation frameworks across equivalent 
roles and performance levels.
•	
Retention & Leadership Mobility: Maintained strong 
retention of female talent through dedicated career 
pathways that consistently elevate high-performing 
women into mid-level and senior leadership positions.
Compliances & Governance
The Company’s commitment to corporate governance 
and employee protection is evidenced by our rigorous 
compliance track record:
•	
Ethics & Security Governance: On time completion of 
the Annual Information Security and Ethics compliance 
survey across all business units.
•	
Statutory & POSH Governance: Complete compliance 
under all statutory state and central labor mandates, 
with affirmative POSH audit certifications across every 
operational branch.
•	
Comprehensive Benefit & Insurance Programs: All 
eligible personnel are covered under comprehensive 
Group Mediclaim (including dependents), Group 
Personal Accident, Group Term Life, and Workmen’s 
Compensation insurance policies.

CORPORATE GOVERNANCE REPORT 2025-26
I.	
COMPANY’S PHILOSOPHY ON CORPORATE 
GOVERNANCE
Corporate governance aims at driving the business 
ethically for creation and enhancement of long-term 
sustainable value for your stakeholders. At Ceinsys, it 
is imperative that the Company’s affairs are managed 
in a fair and transparent manner as it is vital to gain 
and retain the trust of our stakeholders. Ceinsys 
ensures that it evolves and follows not just the stated 
corporate governance guidelines, but also globally 
accepted best practices. The Company’s philosophy 
on corporate governance oversees business strategies 
and ensures fiscal accountability, ethical corporate 
behaviour and fairness to all stakeholders comprising 
employees, investors, customers, regulators, suppliers 
and the society at large. Ceinsys considers its moral 
responsibility to protect the rights of its stakeholders 
and disclose timely, adequate and accurate information 
regarding its financials and performance, as well as the 
leadership and governance of the Company.
The Company’s essential character is shaped by the 
values of transparency, customer satisfaction, integrity, 
professionalism and accountability. The Company 
continuously endeavours to improve on these aspects. 
The Company’s Board of Directors (‘the Board’) view 
Corporate Governance in its widest sense. We are 
committed to defining, following and practicing the 
highest level of corporate governance across all our 
business functions. The main objective is to create 
and adhere to a corporate culture of integrity and 
consciousness. We believe, Corporate Governance 
is not just a destination, but a journey to constantly 
improve sustainable value creation and to achieve the 
upward moving target.
The Company has adopted a comprehensive Code 
of Conduct (‘Code’) for its Directors, KMPs and Senior 
Management, Independent Directors and Employees. 
The Code is available on the Company’s website at 
link: https://cstech.ai/investors/ . Also, the corporate 
policies of the Company are given in Annexure 6 of 
the Board’s Report. It ensures that all directors, senior 
management and employees have the obligation to 
conduct themselves in an honest and ethical manner 
and act in the best interest of the Company at all times.
The Company believes in raising the bar and upholding 
the highest standards of Corporate Governance as it 
enhances the long-term value of the Company for 
its stakeholders. Good governance is an essential 
ingredient of good business. The following report on 
the implementation of the Corporate Governance 
Practices is a sincere effort of the Company to follow 
the Corporate Governance Principles in its letter and 
spirit.
The Company is in compliance with the requirements 
stipulated under Regulations 17 to 27 read with 
Schedule V and clauses (b) to (i) and (t) of sub-
regulation (2) of Regulation 46 of Securities and 
Exchange Board of India (Listing Obligations and 
Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2015 (“SEBI 
Listing Regulations”), as applicable, with regard to 
corporate governance.
II.	
BOARD OF DIRECTORS (“BOARD”)
As on March 31, 2026, the Company has an optimum 
mix of Board which is constituted in compliance with 
the Companies Act, 2013 and SEBI (Listing Obligations 
and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2015 
(“Listing Regulations”) and includes Executive, Non-
Executive & Independent Directors with fifty percent 
of Non-Executive and Independent Directors so as to 
ensure proper governance and management. The Board 
is at the core of corporate governance practice and 
oversees and ensures that the management serves and 
protects the long-term interest of all its stakeholders. 
The Company believes that an active, well-informed 
and independent Board is necessary to ensure the 
highest standards of corporate governance. There 
are no inter-se relationships between the Company’s 
Board members. None of the Independent Director 
hold shares in the Company except Mr. Kishore Dewani 
who holds 35,442 Equity Shares in the Company as on 
March 31, 2026, which is within the threshold limit as 
per Regulation 16(1)(b) of Listing Regulations. There 
has been no resignation of an independent director 
during the reporting year. Further Mr. Prashant Kamat, 
Non-Executive Director Non-Independent Director 
holds 6,81,344 Equity shares of the Company as on 
March 31, 2026.

of the management, direction and performance of 
the Company. The Company’s policy is to maintain 
an optimum combination of Executive and Non-
Executive/Independent Directors. The composition of 
the Company’s Board, which comprised of Nine (9) 
Directors, is given in the table below as on March 31, 
2026, and is in conformity with Regulation 17(1) of the 
Listing Regulations and other applicable regulatory 
requirements. About 50% of the Company’s Board 
comprises of Non-Executive and Independent Directors (IDs). The Board does not comprise of any Nominee Director. 
None of the Directors on the Board:
•	
holds directorships in more than ten public companies;

serves as Director or as Independent Directors in more than seven listed entities; and

who are the Executive Directors serve as independent directors in more than three listed entities.

B.	
Independent Directors
Independent Directors play a key role in the decision-
making process of the Board and in shaping various 
strategic initiatives of the Company. The Independent 
Directors are committed to act in what they believe is in 
the best interests of the Company and its stakeholders. 
The Independent Directors are professionals, with 
expertise and experience in general corporate 
management, science and innovation, realty, public 
policy, business, finance and financial services. This 
wide knowledge in their respective fields of expertise 
and best-in-class boardroom practices helps foster 
varied, unbiased, independent and experienced 
perspective. The Company benefits immensely from 
their inputs in achieving its strategic direction.
Considering the requirements of the skill sets on 
the board, eminent people having an independent 
standing in their respective field and who can 
effectively contribute to the Company’s business and 
policy decisions are considered by the Nomination 
and Remuneration Committee, for appointment as 
Independent Directors on the Board. The Nomination 
and Remuneration Committee, inter alia, considers 
qualification, positive attributes, area of expertise 
and number of Directorships and Memberships held 
in various committees of other companies by such 
persons in accordance with the criteria set for selection 
of Directors and determining Directors’ independence. 
The Board considers the Committee’s recommendation 
and takes appropriate decision.
In terms of Section 149(7) of the Companies Act, 2013, 
the Independent Directors; Mr. Dhruv Kaji, Dr. Satish 
Wate, Mr. Kishore Dewani, Mrs. Maya Swaminathan 
Sinha and Mr. Phaneesh Murthy had given a 
declaration to the Company that they meet the criteria 
of independence as specified under Section 149(6) of 
the Companies Act, 2013. The Board also confirms 
that the independent Directors fulfill the independent 
criteria as prescribed under the Companies Act, 2013 
and Regulation 16(1)(b) of Listing Regulations and are 
Independent of Management of the Company.
The Company has established a Familiarization 
Programme for Independent Directors. The framework 
together with the details of the Familiarization 
Programme conducted has been uploaded on the 
website of the Company. The web-link to this is https://
cstech.ai/investors/
As stipulated by the Code of Independent Directors 
under Schedule IV of the Companies Act, 2013, two (2) 
separate meetings of the Independent Directors of the 
Company were held during the Financial Year 2025-
26 on February 9, 2026 and March 25, 2026, without 
the presence of Non-Independent Directors and 
members of Management, to review the performance 
of Non-Independent Directors, the Board as whole and 
the Chairman of the Company taking into account 
the views of executive directors and non-executive 
directors. They also reviewed the accessibility with 
regards to quality, quantity and timeliness of flow of 
information between the Company, the management 
and the Board that is necessary for the Board to 
effectively and reasonably perform their duties.
During the period under review, Mr. Phaneesh Murthy 
was appointed as Non-executive Independent Director 
of the Company with effect from May 3, 2025. However, 
as on date of this report, Mr. Phaneesh Murthy has 
resigned from the Board of the Company w.e.f. April 28, 
2026, due to personal reasons. He had also confirmed 
that there were no material reasons for his resignation.
C.	
Key Board Proceedings
The Board provides and critically evaluates strategic 
direction of the Company, management policies and 
their effectiveness. Their main function is to ensure 
that long-term interests of the stakeholders are being 
served. The agenda for the Board includes strategic 
review from each of the Board committees, a detailed 
analysis and review of annual strategic and operating 
plans and capital allocation and budgets. The Board 
holds minimum four meetings every year to review 
and discuss the performance of the Company, its 
future plans, strategies and other pertinent issues 
relating to the Company. The Board has complete 
access to all the relevant information of the Company. 
The quantum and quality of information supplied by 
the management to the Board goes well beyond the 
minimum requirement stipulated under Companies 
Act, 2013 and SEBI (Listing Obligations and Disclosure 
Requirements) Regulations, 2015.
To enable the Board to discharge its responsibilities 
properly, the directors are effectively briefed at every 
Board Meeting. Senior Management members are 
also invited, as and when the need arise, to attend the 
Board Meetings and to provide additional inputs on 
the items being discussed by the Board. Apart from 
placing the statutory information required before 
the Board Members, it is the policy of the Company 
to regularly place the information/ matter involving 
major decisions like Annual Budget, Business Plans, 
Operations, Technology Collaboration, Investments, 
Half yearly results and quarterly compliance reports on 
various laws applicable to the Company, legal matters 
and other material information.
The Board performs the following specific functions in 
addition to overseeing the business and management:
a.	
Review, monitor and approve major financial and 
business strategies and corporate actions;
b.	
Assess critical risks facing the Company – review 
options for their mitigation;
c.	
Provide counsel on the selection, evaluation, 
development and compensation of senior 
management;

1.	
Board Meetings and Deliberations
The Board meets at least four times a year as 
per the law. Apart from the four Board meetings, 
additional Board meetings are also convened by 
giving appropriate notice to address the specific 
requirements of the Company. However, in case 
of a special and urgent business need, the Board’s 
approval is taken by passing resolutions by 
circulation, as permitted by law, which are noted 
and confirmed in the subsequent Board Meeting. 
During the period under review i.e. April 1, 2025 to 
March 31, 2026, Seven (7) Board Meetings were 
held on May 3, 2025; July 30, 2025; September 
3, 2025; November 6, 2025; December 16, 2025; 
February 9, 2026; and March 25, 2026. The gap 
between two Meetings did not exceed one hundred 
and twenty days. The requisite quorum was present 
in all the meetings. Resolutions were also passed 
by circulation in compliance with the provisions of 
Companies Act, 2013 and Rules thereunder.
	
2.	
Information Material
The dates of Board Meeting were decided 
well in advance and notices of such meetings 
were circulated. Agenda and notes on agenda 
were circulated to Board in a defined format in 
advance and few supporting papers were placed 
before the Board at the time of Meeting. Material 
information was incorporated in the agenda to 
facilitate meaningful and focused discussions 
at the meeting. Where it was not practical to 
attach any document to the agenda, it was tabled 
before the meeting, with specific reference to this 
effect on the agenda. In special and exceptional 
circumstances, additional or supplementary 
item(s) on the agenda are permitted with the 
approval of the Chairman.
	
3. 	
Minutes
The Company Secretary records minutes of 
proceedings of meeting of Board and Committees 
thereof. Draft minutes are circulated to Board/ 
Committee members for their comments. The 
minutes are entered in the Minutes Book well 
within the stipulated time, after considering the 
comments of Board members.

5.	
The Committees of the Board
The Board Committees play a crucial role in the 
governance structure of the Company and have 
been constituted to deal with specific areas / 
activities as mandated by applicable regulations 
which concern the Company and need a closer 
review. The Board Committees are set up under 
the formal approval of the Board to carry out 
clearly defined roles which are considered to be 
performed by Members of the Board, as a part 
of good governance practice. The Chairman of 
the respective Committees informs the Board 
about the summary of the discussions held in 
the Committee Meetings. The minutes of the 
meetings of all Committees are placed before 
the Board for review. The Board Committees can 
request special invitees to join the meeting, as 
appropriate.

A.	
Audit Committee
The Company has an adequately qualified Audit 
Committee and its composition meets the 
requirement of Section 177 of the Companies 
Act, 2013 and Regulation 18 of SEBI (Listing 
Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) 
Regulations, 2015. The Audit Committee 
also acts as a link between the statutory and 
internal auditors and the Board of Directors. 
Its purpose is to assist the Board in fulfilling its 
oversight responsibilities of monitoring financial 
reporting processes, reviewing the Company’s 
established systems and processes for internal 
financial controls, governance and reviewing the 
Company’s statutory and internal audit activities. 
The Audit Committee ensures prudent financial 
and accounting practices, fiscal discipline and 
transparency in financial reporting. In terms of 
one of its important charter, the quarterly financial 
statements are reviewed by the Audit Committee 
and recommended to the Board for its adoption. 
The Audit Committee was constituted on July 26, 
2014.
In addition to the Audit Committee members, 
Statutory Auditors, the Chief Financial Officer, 
Head – Internal Audit and Divisional Heads are 
also invited to the Audit Committee Meetings on 
need basis.
	
1.	
The brief terms of reference of Audit Committee 
are as under:
a.	
Overseeing the Company’s financial 
reporting process and the disclosure of 
its financial information to ensure that the 
financial statements are correct, sufficient 
and credible.
b.	
Recommending 
to 
the 
Board, 
the 
appointment, re-appointment and, if 
required, the replacement or removal of the 
statutory auditor and the fixation of audit 
fees;
c.	
Approving payment to statutory auditors for 
any other services rendered by the statutory 
auditors;
d.	
Reviewing, with the management, the Annual 
Financial Statements and Auditor’s report 
thereon before submission to the Board for 
approval, with particular reference to:
i.	
matters required to be included in the 
Director ‘s Responsibility Statement to 
be included in the Board ‘s report in 
terms of Section 134 of the Companies 
Act 2013;
ii.	
changes, if any, in accounting policies 
and practices along with reasons for the 
same;
iii.	
major accounting entries involving 
estimates based on the exercise of 
judgement by management;
iv.	
significant adjustments made in the 
financial statements arising out of audit 
findings;
v.	
compliance with listing and other 
legal requirements relating to financial 
statements;
vi.	
disclosure of any related party 
transactions; and
vii.	 qualifications in the Audit Report/ 
modified opinion(s) in the draft audit 
report.
e.	
Reviewing, with the management, the 
quarterly Financial Statements before 
submission to the board for approval;
f.	
Reviewing, with the management, the 
statement of uses / application of funds 
raised through an issue (public issue, rights 
issue, preferential issue, etc.), the statement of 
funds utilized for purposes other than those 
stated in the offer document/prospectus/
notice and the report submitted by the 
monitoring agency monitoring the utilization 
of proceeds of a public or rights issue, and 
making appropriate recommendations to the 
Board to take up steps in this matter;
g.	
Reviewing and monitoring the auditor’s 
independence and performance, and 
effectiveness of audit process;
h.	
Approval or any subsequent modification of 
transactions of the listed entity with related 
parties;
i.	
Scrutinizing of inter-corporate loans and 
investments;
j.	
Evaluating value of undertakings or assets of 
the listed entity, wherever it is necessary;
k.	
Evaluation of internal financial controls and 
risk management systems;
l.	
Reviewing, 
with 
the 
management, 
performance of statutory and internal 
auditors, and adequacy of the internal control 
systems;
m.	
Reviewing the adequacy of internal audit 
function, if any, including the structure 
of the internal audit department, staffing 
and seniority of the official heading the 
department, reporting structure coverage 
and frequency of internal audit;
n.	
Discussing with the internal auditors any 
significant findings and follow up there on;
o.	
Reviewing the findings of any internal 
investigations by the internal auditors into 
matters where there is suspected fraud or 
irregularity or a failure of internal control 
systems of a material nature and reporting 
the matter to the Board;
p.	
Discussing with the statutory auditors before 
the audit commences, about the nature 
and scope of audit as well as post-audit 
discussion to ascertain any area of concern;
q.	
Looking into the reasons for substantial

B.	
Nomination and Remuneration Committee 
(NRC)
In compliance with the provisions of Section 
178 of the Companies Act, 2013 and Regulation 
19 of SEBI (Listing Obligations and Disclosure 
Requirements) Regulations, 2015, the Board 
of Directors of the Company has constituted 
Nomination and Remuneration Committee on July 
26, 2014. The Board has approved a Nomination 
and Remuneration Policy for Directors, KMPs and 
other Senior Management.
The committee believes that sound succession 
planning of the senior leadership is the most 
important ingredient for creating a robust 
future for the Company. Therefore, the NRC has 
adopted a rigorous process to ensure that the 
Board selects the right candidates for senior 
leadership positions. The Chairman of the NRC is 
an Independent Director.
	
1.	
The brief terms of reference of Nomination and 
Remuneration Committee are as under:
a.	
Formulating of the criteria for determining 
qualifications, positive attributes and 
independence of a director and recommend 
to the board of directors a policy relating 
to, the remuneration of the directors, key 
managerial personnel and other employees;
b.	
For every appointment of an independent 
director, evaluate the balance of skills, 
knowledge and experience on the Board 
and on the basis of such evaluation, prepare 
a description of the role and capabilities 
required of an independent director. The 
person recommended to the Board for 
appointment as an independent director 
shall have the capabilities identified in such 
description. For the purpose of identifying 
suitable candidates, the Committee may 
use the services of an external agencies, 
if required; consider candidates from a 
wide range of backgrounds, having due 
regard to diversity; and consider the time 
commitments of the candidates;
c.	
Formulating of criteria for evaluation of 
performance of independent directors and 
the board of directors;
d.	
Devising a policy on diversity of board of 
directors;
e.	
Identifying persons who are qualified to 
become directors and who may be appointed 
in senior management in accordance with 
the criteria laid down, and recommend to 
the board of directors their appointment and 
removal.
f.	
Evaluating and to recommend whether to 
extend or continue the term of appointment 
of the independent director, on the basis 
of the report of performance evaluation of 
independent directors.
g.	
Determine our Company’s policy on specific 
remuneration package for the Managing 
Director/Executive Director including pension 
rights.
h.	
Evaluating and to recommend salary, 
allowances, perquisites, bonuses, notice 
period, severance fees and increment of 
Executive Directors.
i.	
Define and implement the Performance 
Linked Incentive Scheme (including ESOP of 
the Company) and evaluate the performance 
and determine the amount of incentive of the 
Executive Directors for that purpose.
j.	
Evaluating and to recommend the amount 
of Commission payable to the Whole time 
Directors, if any.
k.	
Review and suggest revision of the total 
remuneration package of the Executive 
Directors keeping in view the performance 
of the Company, standards prevailing in the 
industry, statutory guidelines etc;
l.	
To formulate and administer the Employee 
Stock Option Scheme.
m.	
To recommend to Board, all remuneration 
in whatever form, payable to senior 
management.
